Assume without loss of generality that
M/p_{i},M/p_{j}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
. By the assumptions of the lemma, we also have
\Phi_{M}|A
. Applying Lemma
4.9
(ii) to
A
, we get that
A
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction, as claimed.
∎

Next, we discuss cyclotomic divisibility.
Since
F_{i}(X)=(X^{M}-1)/(X^{M/p_{i}}-1)
, we have

(9.2)

\Phi_{s}|F_{i}\ \Leftrightarrow\ p_{i}^{2}|s|M,

and similarly for
F_{j}
and
F_{k}
. In particular,

(9.3)

\prod_{\alpha_{i}=1}^{2}\Phi_{M/p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}}(X)\prod_{\alpha_{j}=1}^{2}\Phi_{M/p_{j}^{\alpha_{j}}}(X)\Big|\mathcal{K}(X),

and similarly for other permutations of the indices
i,j,k
.

Lemma 9.8
.

Assume (F’). For each
\alpha_{k}\in\{1,2\}
, we have

\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|A\ \Leftrightarrow\ \Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|\mathcal{K}.

In particular, if
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}\nmid A
for some
\alpha_{k}\in\{1,2\}
, then
\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
.
Similar conclusions hold for other permutations of the indices
i,j,k
.

Proof.

The lemma follows immediately from (
9.3
) if
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}
are mutually disjoint, since then we have
A(X)=\mathcal{I}(X)+\mathcal{J}(X)+\mathcal{K}(X)
. In the general case, we need a mild workaround as follows.

Write
A(X)=\mathcal{I}^{\prime}(X)+\mathcal{J}^{\prime}(X)+\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(X)
, where the sets
\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are pairwise disjoint and
\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
are
M
-fibered in the
p_{i},p_{j}
, and
p_{k}
direction, respectively. This can be done by
splitting up
\mathbb{Z}_{M}
into pairwise disjoint
D(M)
-grids
\Lambda_{\tau}
and adding
A\cap\Lambda_{\tau}
to one of
\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
, according to the direction in which
A\cap\Lambda_{\tau}
is
M
-fibered. (If
A\cap\Lambda_{\tau}
is fibered in more than one direction, choose one arbitrarily and add
A\cap\Lambda_{\tau}
to the corresponding set.)

It follows from (
9.3
) that
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|A
if and only if
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
. To pass from
\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
to
\mathcal{K}
, we write
\mathcal{K}(X)=\mathcal{K}^{\prime}(X)+\mathcal{K}_{i}(X)+\mathcal{K}_{j}(X)
, where
\mathcal{K}_{i}\subset\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{K}_{j}\subset\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
. By Lemma
9.5
,
\mathcal{K}_{i}
is a union of pairwise disjoint sets of the form
a*F_{i}*F_{k}
, where
a\in A
. In particular, by (
9.3
) we have
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|\mathcal{K}_{i}(X)
. Applying the same argument to
\mathcal{K}_{j}
, we see that
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|\mathcal{K}^{\prime}
if and only if
\Phi_{M/p_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}}|\mathcal{K}
, and the lemma follows.
∎

We finish with two counting lemmas.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
A\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M},M=p_{i}^{2}p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2},|A|=|B|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
. Then

(9.4)

\{m/p_{k}^{\alpha}:\ \alpha\in\{0,1,2\},m\in\{M,M/p_{i},M/p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{j}\}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)\neq\{M\}.

Proof.

Suppose that (
9.4
) fails. It follows that any
M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}^{2}
-grid may contain at most one element of
B
. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{M}
is a disjoint union of
p_{i}p_{j}
such grids, it follows that
|B|\leq M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}^{2}
, contradicting our assumption that
|B|=M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
.
∎

Lemma 9.10
.

Assume that (F’) holds.

(i) Let
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
and
\Pi_{k}:=\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{\alpha_{i}})
for some
\alpha_{i}\in\{1,2\}
.
Suppose that
|A\cap\Pi_{k}|=p_{j}p_{k}
and
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi_{k}=\emptyset
. Then
A\cap\Pi_{k}\subset\mathcal{K}
.

(ii) Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
and
\Pi_{i}:=\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
.
Suppose that

(9.5)

|A\cap\Pi_{i}|=p_{j}p_{k}

and
A\cap\Pi_{i}\subset\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{K}
. Then
p_{i}<p_{j}
.

The same conclusions hold with
j
and
k
interchanged.

Proof.

(i)
Suppose first that
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{k}\neq\emptyset
, and let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{k}
. Then
a\not\in\mathcal{I}
. By Lemma
9.5
(i), we have
A\cap\Pi_{k}=a*F_{j}*F_{k}
, so that
A\cap\Pi_{k}\subset\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{k}=\emptyset
. Since
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
,
there exists a nonnegative integer
c_{j}
and a positive integer
c_{k}
such that

p_{j}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi_{k}|=c_{j}p_{j}+c_{k}p_{k}.

This clearly implies
c_{j}=0
and
c_{k}=p_{j}
, thus proving
\mathcal{J}\cap\Pi_{k}=\emptyset
.

(ii) Assume first

(9.6)

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{i}\neq\emptyset,

and let
a\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{i}
. Denote
\Lambda_{0}=\Lambda(a,D(M))
. By assumption,
A\cap\Lambda_{0}
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{\nu}
direction for some
\nu\in\{i,j,k\}
. Suppose that the latter holds with
\nu=j
. Since
a*F_{k}\subset A\cap\Lambda_{0}
, in fact we have
a*F_{k}*F_{j}\subset A\cap\Lambda_{0}
. But
a*F_{i}
is also contained in
A\cap\Lambda_{0}
, hence

|A\cap\Pi_{i}|\geq|A\cap\Lambda_{0}|\geq p_{j}p_{k}+p_{i}-1>p_{j}p_{k},

thus contradicting (
9.5
). Interchanging
j
and
k
, the same argument shows
A\cap\Lambda_{0}
cannot be
M
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction.

It follows that
A\cap\Lambda_{0}
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction. Since
(a*F_{j})\cup(a*F_{k})\subset A\cap\Lambda_{0}
, the set
A\cap\Lambda_{0}
must contain a structure as in (
9.1
). By (
9.5
)

p_{j}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi_{i}|\geq|A\cap\Lambda_{0}|>p_{i}p_{k},

which is what we wanted to show.

For the rest of the proof we assume (
9.6
) fails. We show that
A\cap\Pi_{i}
can be written (not necessarily uniquely) as a disjoint union of
M
-fibers in the
p_{i}
and
p_{k}
directions. Indeed, if
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi_{i}
and
\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi_{i}
are disjoint, we are done. Assume now that

(9.7)

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda\neq\emptyset,

for some
D(M)
-grid
\Lambda\subset\Pi_{i}
. From the failure of (
9.6
), we deduce that
A\cap\Lambda
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{\nu}
direction, with either
\nu=i
or
\nu=k
. By Lemma
9.5
(i),
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda
is a union of pairwise disjoint cosets of
F_{i}*F_{k}
. We write all such cosets as unions of
M
-fibers in the
p_{i}
direction. Then, we add all the remaining
M
-fibers in the
p_{i}
and
p_{k}
directions in
A\cap\Lambda
, disjoint from
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda
and from each other. Since
\Lambda
is an arbitrary grid satisfying (
9.7
), the claim follows.

Since
A\cap\Pi_{i}
can be written a disjoint union of
M
-fibers in the
p_{i}
and
p_{k}
directions, there must exist a positive integer
c_{i}
and a nonnegative integer
c_{k}
such that

p_{j}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi_{i}|=c_{i}p_{i}+c_{k}p_{k}.

It follows that
c_{i}=c^{\prime}_{i}p_{k}
for some positive integer
c^{\prime}_{i}
. But then
p_{j}=c^{\prime}_{i}p_{i}+c_{k}
, so that
p_{j}>p_{i}
as claimed.
∎

9.3.
Fibering on lower scales

Lemma 9.11
.

Assume that (F) holds.

(i) Let
\Lambda:=\Lambda(a_{0},D(N_{i}))
for some
a_{0}\in\mathcal{I}
. If
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\Lambda=\emptyset
and
A
is
N_{i}
-fibered on
\Lambda
, it cannot be fibered in the
p_{j}
direction.

(ii) If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|B
and

(9.8)

\{M/p_{j},M/p_{k},M/p_{i}p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{k}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)=\emptyset,

then
B
must be
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction. Note in particular that if
\{D(M)|m|M\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
, then (
9.8
) holds.

Proof.

(i) Assume, by contradiction, that
A
is
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction on
\Lambda
.
Then
\mathbb{A}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[a]=p_{i}
and
\mathbb{A}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{j}}[a]=p_{i}\phi(p_{j})
for all
a\in\mathcal{I}\cap\Lambda
, meaning that
\mathcal{I}\cap\Lambda
is also
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction. This contradicts the assumption that
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\Lambda
is empty.

(ii) By (
9.8
), we have
\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{j}}[b]=\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{k}}[b]=0
for all
b\in B
. It follows from Lemma
4.9
(ii) that
B
is
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction.
∎

By Lemma
9.8
, if
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
, then
\Phi_{N_{k}}|\mathcal{K}
.
We consider the question of whether, in these circumstances,
\mathcal{K}
is permitted to have an unfibered grid on a lower scale.

Lemma 9.12
.

Assume that (F) holds. Suppose that
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
and that there exists a
D(N_{k})
-grid on which
\mathcal{K}
is not fibered. Then:

•

\{D(M)|m|M\}\cup\{M/p_{k}^{2},M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}^{2}\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
,

•

there exists an
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
such that
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})
.

Proof.

Assume that
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
. By Lemma
9.8
, we have

\Phi_{N_{k}}|\mathcal{K}
. Consider
\mathcal{K}
as a multiset in
\mathbb{Z}_{N_{k}}
with constant multiplicity
p_{k}
. We claim that if
\mathcal{K}
is not fibered on a
D(N_{k})
-grid
\Lambda
, it must satisfy the conclusion of either Lemma
6.2
or Lemma
6.3
with
N=N_{k}
on that grid. Indeed, if this is not the case, then we must have

\{m:\ D(N_{k})|m|N_{k}\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}_{N_{k}}(\mathcal{K}).

But then, for every
D(N_{k})|m|N_{k}
there exist
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
(depending on
m
) such that
(a-a^{\prime},N_{k})=m
. Since
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}}[a]=\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}}[a^{\prime}]=\phi(p_{k})
, we have

\big\{m/p_{k}^{\alpha}:\ \alpha\in\{0,1,2\},m\in\{M,M/p_{i},M/p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{j}\}\big\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(\mathcal{K}).

The latter contradicts Lemma
9.9
.

On the other hand, suppose that
\mathcal{K}\in\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{Z}_{N_{k}})
has at least
p_{j}+1
distinct points in some plane
\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})
in
\mathbb{Z}_{N_{k}}
, each of multiplicity
p_{k}
. Then
|A\cap\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})|\geq(p_{j}+1)p_{k}
in
\mathbb{Z}_{M}
,
contradicting Lemma
4.3
. The same argument applies with
i
and
j
interchanged.

Among the structures described in Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
with
N=N_{k}
, the only ones that avoid configurations as in the last paragraph are as follows.

•

\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda
has the
p_{k}
full plane structure as in Lemma
6.2
, so that for some
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
we have
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})
and
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{i}p_{j}}[x]=p_{k}\phi(p_{i}p_{j})
.

•

\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda
has a
p_{i}
or
p_{j}
corner structure as in Lemma
6.3
(i).

•

\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda
has a
p_{i}
or
p_{j}
almost corner structure as in Lemma
6.3
(ii), so that (possibly after a permutation of
i
and
j
) there exist
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
such that
(x_{\nu}-x_{\nu^{\prime}},N_{k})=N_{k}/p_{i}
for
\nu\neq\nu^{\prime}
,

\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x_{1}]=\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x_{2}]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})
and
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{j}}[x_{3}]=\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{j}}[x_{4}]=p_{k}\phi(p_{j})
.

We address the second case, the first and third case being similar.
Indeed, a
p_{i}
corner structure in
\mathcal{K}
on the
N_{k}
scale means that there exist
a,a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
with
(a-a^{\prime},N_{k})=N_{k}/p_{i}
so that

(9.9)

\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[a]=\mathbb{K}_{M/p_{k}^{2}}[a]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})

and

(9.10)

\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{j}}[a^{\prime}]=\mathbb{K}_{M/p_{j}}[a^{\prime}]+\mathbb{K}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}[a^{\prime}]=p_{k}\phi(p_{j}).

Now consider
\mathcal{K}
on scale
M
.
We have
(a-a^{\prime},M)\in\{M/p_{i},M/p_{i}p_{k}\}
, with the fiber chain in (
9.9
) attached to
a
.
By (
9.10
) and the fact that
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
, we also have
a^{\prime}*F_{j}*F_{k}\subset\mathcal{K}
. Hence the conclusions of the lemma hold with
x=a
.
∎

Corollary 9.13
.

Assume (F). If
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
and
p_{k}>\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
, then
\mathcal{K}
is
N_{k}
-fibered on each
D(N_{k})
-grid in one of the
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
directions. In particular,
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

Assume without loss of generality that
p_{i}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
. By Lemma
9.8
,
we have
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
if and only if
\Phi_{N_{k}}|\mathcal{K}
. By Lemma
9.12
, if there exists a
D(N_{k})
-grid on which
\mathcal{K}
is unfibered, then there must exist
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
such that
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})
, thus

\displaystyle|A\cap\Pi(x,p_{j}^{2})|

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})

\displaystyle>p_{i}p_{k}

which contradicts Lemma
4.3
. Thus
\mathcal{K}
must be fibered on all
D(N_{k})
-grids. On the other hand, by the same argument as above,
\mathcal{K}
cannot be
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction on any
D(N_{k})
-grid.
∎

9.4.
Proof of Proposition
9.2

In this section, we are assuming (F1), which we state here again for the reader’s convenience.

Assumption (F1).
We have
A\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, where
M=p_{i}^{2}p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}
is odd. Furthermore,
|A|=|B|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
,
\Phi_{M}|A
,
A
is fibered on
D(M)
-grids,
and

(9.11)

\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}\hbox{ are pairwise disjoint.}

We must prove that at least one of the sets
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}
or
\mathcal{K}
has to be empty.
To this end we assume the contrary, i.e.,

(9.12)

\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J},\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset,

and prove by contradiction that (
9.12
) cannot hold.
We may assume, without loss of generality, that

(9.13)

p_{i}<p_{j}<p_{k}.

By Corollary
9.13
, this implies that
\Phi_{N_{j}}
and
\Phi_{N_{k}}
cannot divide
A
, so that

(9.14)

\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{N_{k}}|B.

We start with a cyclotomic divisibility result.

Proposition 9.14
.

Assume that (F1), (
9.12
), and (
9.14
) hold. Then
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

The proof is divided into several steps. In each of the following claims, the assumptions of the proposition are assumed to hold.

Claim 1.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
, then
\mathcal{I}
is
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction, so that
for every
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
,

(9.15)

\mathbb{I}_{M}[a_{i}]+\mathbb{I}_{M/p_{i}}[a_{i}]+\mathbb{I}_{M/p_{i}^{2}}[a_{i}]=p_{i}^{2}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\mathcal{I}
is
N_{i}
-fibered on each
D(N_{i})
-grid. Once we know that, Lemma
9.11
(i) together with (
9.11
) implies that the
N_{i}
-fibering must be in the
p_{i}
direction, and the claim follows.

Assume, by contradiction, that there exists a
D(N_{i})
-grid over which
\mathcal{I}
is not fibered. By Lemma
9.12
,

(9.16)

\{D(M)|m|M\}\cup\{M/p_{i}^{2},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{j}p_{k}\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A)

and there exists
x_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with

(9.17)

\mathbb{I}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{i}}[x_{0}]=\phi(p_{i}^{2}).

The proof of Lemma
9.12
implies further that
\mathcal{I}\cap\Lambda(x_{0},D(N_{i}))
must contain one of the structures described in Lemmas
6.2
and
6.3
with
N=N_{i}
. Additionally, (
9.14
) and Lemma
9.11
(ii) imply that
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction and
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction, hence

(9.18)

M/p_{j}^{2},M/p_{k}^{2},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

We claim that

(9.19)

\mathbb{I}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[x_{0}]=0.

Suppose this is not true, then

(9.20)

\mathbb{A}_{M}[x_{0}]+\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}}[x_{0}]+\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}^{2}}[x_{0}]=p_{i}^{2}.

Let
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
and
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
. Recall from Lemma
2.11
that the fibering in
B
allows one to shift the fibers rooted at
a_{j}
and
a_{k}
by distance
M/p_{j}^{2}
and
M/p_{k}^{2}
, respectively. Let
x_{j},x_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with

(a_{\nu}-x_{\nu},M)=M/p_{\nu}^{2}\ \text{ and }\ p_{\nu}^{2}|x_{0}-x_{\nu}\ \text{ for }\ \nu\in\{j,k\}.

Let
A^{\prime}
be the set obtained by shifting the fibers rooted at
a_{j}
and
a_{k}
to
x_{j}
and
x_{k}
respectively, so that
x_{j}*F_{j},x_{k}*F_{k}\subset A^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.11
,
A^{\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
and
A^{\prime}
is T2-equivalent to
A
.

Let
a_{1},a_{2}\in\ell_{i}(x_{0})
be the points with
p_{i}^{2}|a_{1}-x_{j}
,
p_{i}^{2}|a_{2}-x_{k}
. By (
9.20
),
\ell_{i}(x_{0})\subset A\cap A^{\prime}
. It follows from (
9.18
) that we cannot have
M/p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A^{\prime})
, hence

(a_{1}-x_{j},M)=M/p_{k}
. Similarly,
(a_{2}-x_{k},M)=M/p_{j}
.
But now
M/p_{i}^{2}p_{j},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A^{\prime})
, hence
M/p_{i}^{2}p_{j},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
. Together with (
9.16
), this contradicts Lemma
9.9
. This proves (
9.19
).

We therefore conclude that
\mathcal{I}\cap\Lambda(x_{0},D(N_{i}))
has either a full plane structure (Lemma
6.2
) or an almost corner structure (Lemma
6.3
(ii)).
In either one of these cases, there exists a point

(9.21)

a_{i}^{*}\in\mathcal{I}\cap\Lambda(x_{0},D(M))

such that for each
\nu\in\{j,k\}
,
A\cap(a_{i}^{*}*F_{i}*F_{\nu})
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction, but is not
M
-fibered in the
p_{\nu}
direction.

Let
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
, and let
x_{j}\in\ell_{j}(a_{j})
be the point such that
p_{j}^{2}|x_{0}-x_{j}
. We consider two cases.

(a)

If it is possible to choose
a_{j}
so that

x_{j}\in a_{j}*F_{j}
, we fix that choice, and let
A^{\prime}:=A
.

(b)

Otherwise, let
A^{\prime}
be the set obtained from
A
by shifting the fiber
a_{j}*F_{j}
to
x_{j}
if necessary, so that
x_{j}*F_{j}\subset A^{\prime}
. By Lemma
2.11
,
A^{\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
and
A^{\prime}
is T2-equivalent to
A
.
Since we are not in case (a),

A^{\prime}\cap(x_{j}*F_{i}*F_{j})
contains no other
M
-fibers in the
p_{j}
direction.

We show that either
x_{j}\in\Lambda(x_{0},D(M))
or

(9.22)

M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

•

Suppose that
p_{i}|x_{0}-x_{j}
. If
(x_{0}-x_{j},M)\in\{M,M/p_{i},M/p_{k},M/p_{i}p_{k}\}
, then clearly
x_{j}\in\Lambda(x_{0},D(M))
. If on the other hand
(x_{0}-x_{j},M)\in\{M/p_{k}^{2},M/p_{i}p_{k}^{2}\}
,
then by (
9.21
), there exists a fiber
a*F_{i}\subset\mathcal{I}
with
a\in\Lambda(x_{0},D(M))
and
p_{i}^{2}|a-x_{j}
.
Then
M/p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(a,x_{j}*F_{j})\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A^{\prime})
, contradicting (
9.18
).

•

Assume now that
p_{i}\nmid x_{0}-x_{j}
. If
(x_{0}-x_{j},M)=M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}
, then by (
9.17
) together with the fact that
p_{i}\geq 3
, there must be an
a\in A
with
(a-x_{0},M)=M/p_{i}^{2}
and
(a-x_{j},M)=M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}
, proving (
9.22
) since
x_{j}\in A^{\prime}
. Otherwise, we have
(x_{0}-x_{j},M)=M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}^{2}
, but then by (
9.17
), there must be
a\in A
with
(a-x_{0},M)=M/p_{i}^{2}
and
(a-x_{j},M)=M/p_{k}^{2}
, contradicting (
9.18
).

Suppose now that
x_{j}\in\Lambda(x_{0},D(M))
. We claim that, in this case,
A^{\prime}
contains a
p_{k}
extended corner structure
consisting of the fiber in
x_{j}*F_{i}*F_{j}
in the
p_{j}
direction and
at least one fiber in
a_{i}^{*}*F_{i}*F_{j}
in the
p_{i}
direction. Indeed, by
(
9.21
) and (
9.11
), we have

\Lambda(x_{j},D(M))\cap(\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K})=\emptyset.

In particular, we must be in case (b) above, and
A\cap(x_{j}*F_{i}*F_{j})=x_{j}*F_{j}
.
This, together with the choice of
a_{i}^{*}
, proves that the conditions of Definition
5.4
(ii) hold.

In that case, however, we proved in Theorem
8.1
that
A^{\prime}
(therefore
A
) is T2-equivalent to a
D(M)
-grid. It follows that
A
satisfies (T2), therefore
A\oplus B^{\flat}=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, where
\Phi_{p_{\nu}}|B^{\flat}
for all
\nu\in\{i,j,k\}
. This contradicts (
9.16
), since clearly
M/p_{i}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B^{\flat})
.

We are therefore left with (
9.22
). By the same argument with
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
interchanged, we
must also have
M/p_{i}^{2}p_{j}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
. Together with (
9.16
), this again contradicts Lemma
9.9
.
∎

Claim 2.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
, then
\Phi_{M_{i}}\nmid A
, and therefore
\Phi_{M_{i}}|B
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.8
, if
\Phi_{M_{i}}|A
, then
\Phi_{M_{i}}|\mathcal{I}
. Since
M_{i}
has only 2 distinct prime divisors,
we may apply Lemma
4.7
on the scale
M_{i}
. We conclude that every element of
\mathcal{I}
belongs to either an
M_{i}
-fiber in the
p_{j}
direction, in which case we have
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|\geq p_{i}^{2}p_{j}
, or to an
M_{i}
-fiber in the
p_{k}
direction, in which case we have
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{j}^{2})|\geq p_{i}^{2}p_{k}
. Since both bounds contradict Lemma
4.3
, we deduce
\Phi_{M_{i}}|B
, and the lemma follows.
∎

The next claim is a direct consequence of Claim 2, (
9.15
), and Lemma
4.7
.

Claim 3.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
, then
\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[y]\in\{0,1\}
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
. Moreover, for every
b\in B
, either

(9.23)

\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b^{\prime}]=\phi(p_{j})\text{ for all }b^{\prime}\in B\cap\Lambda(b,D(M_{i})),

and, since
p_{i}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
,

(9.24)

M/p_{i}p_{j},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{j}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B),

or

(9.25)

\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{k}}[b^{\prime}]=\phi(p_{k})\text{ for all }b^{\prime}\in B\cap\Lambda(b,D(M_{i}))

and

(9.26)

M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

Claim 4.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
, then for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
we have

\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[a_{i}]=\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{k}}[a_{i}]=0
.

Proof.

If there exist
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
such that (
9.23
) holds with
b^{\prime}=b_{1}
and (
9.25
) holds with
b^{\prime}=b_{2}
, then the claim follows from (
9.24
) and (
9.26
). Assume therefore that (
9.23
) holds for all
b\in B
, and, consequently,
\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[a_{i}]=0
for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{k}}[a_{i}]>0
for some
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.
It follows from (
9.15
) that

(9.27)

M/p_{i},M/p_{k},M/p_{i}^{2},M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A).

We claim that

(9.28)

M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

Assuming this, we prove Claim 4 as follows. By (
9.12
), (
9.27
), and
(
9.28
), we have
N_{k}/p_{i},N_{k}/p_{j}\not\in{\mathrm{Div}}_{N_{k}}(B)
.
By (
9.14
) and Lemma
4.9
(ii), B must be
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction, so that

\mathbb{B}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[b]=\phi(p_{k})\ \ \forall b\in B.

Let
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
. Since
a_{i}
satisfies (
9.15
), we may assume (moving
a_{i}
to a different point in the same fiber chain if necessary) that

(9.29)

p_{i}^{2}|a_{k}-a_{i}.

Then the pair
(A,B)
has a (1,2)-cofibered structure, with
a_{k}*F_{k}
as a cofiber.
Let
x^{\prime}_{k}\in\ell_{k}(a_{k})
be the point such that
p_{k}^{2}|a_{i}-x^{\prime}_{k}
. If
p_{k}|a_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k}
, we note that
x^{\prime}_{k}\in A
, and let
A^{\prime}:=A
. If on the other hand
(a_{k}-x^{\prime}_{k},M)=M/p_{k}^{2}
, we use
Lemma
2.11
to shift
a_{k}*F_{k}
to
x^{\prime}_{k}
, obtaining a new set
A^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}_{k}\in A^{\prime}
,
A^{\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, and
A^{\prime}
is T2-equivalent to
A
.

By (
9.24
) and (
9.29
), we must have
(a_{i}-x^{\prime}_{k},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}
, so that
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A^{\prime})
. In particular,
M/p_{j}^{2}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
. On the other hand, we also have
\Phi_{N_{j}}|B
, and

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{k}}[b]=\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{j}}[b]=0\text{ for all }b\in B.

By Lemma
4.9
, B must be
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction, so that

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]=\phi(p_{i})
for every
b\in B
. Together with (
9.12
), this means that every grid
\Lambda(b,M/p_{i}p_{j})
with
b\in B
contains exactly
p_{i}
points of
B
.
On the other hand, the assumption that (
9.23
) holds for all
b\in B
implies that
every such grid contains exactly
p_{j}
points of
B
. This contradiction proves the claim, assuming (
9.28
).

We now prove (
9.28
). Assume, by contradiction, that
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
(b-b^{\prime},M)=M/p_{j}p_{k}
. Let
y,y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus B
with
(b-y,M)=(b^{\prime}-y^{\prime},M)=M/p_{k}
,
(b-y^{\prime},M)=(b^{\prime}-y,M)=M/p_{j}
, and consider the saturating set
B_{y,a_{i}}
. Applying Corollary
2.9
(i) to
B_{y}
, once with respect to
b
and again with respect to
b^{\prime}
, we get

B_{y,a_{i}}\subset\ell_{i}(b)\cup\ell_{i}(y)\cup\ell_{i}(b^{\prime})\cup\ell_{i}(y^{\prime}).

If
B_{y,a_{i}}\cap(\ell_{i}(y)\cup\ell_{i}(y^{\prime}))
is nonempty, then
\{M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{i}^{2}p_{k}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
must be nonempty, while if
B_{y,a_{i}}\cap(\ell_{i}(b)\cup\ell_{i}(b^{\prime}))
is nonempty, then
\{M/p_{i},M/p_{i}^{2}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
must be nonempty. Both of these contradict (
9.27
).
∎

Claim 5.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
and
M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, then there must exist
b_{j},b_{k}\in B
such that (
9.23
) holds with
b=b_{j}
and (
9.25
) holds with
b=b_{k}
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that the conclusion is false. Without loss of generality, we may assume that (
9.23
) holds for all
b\in B
.

Since
M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, we have
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{k}}[b]=0
for all
b\in B
. By (
9.14
) and Lemma
4.9
, B must be fibered on
D(N_{j})
-grids, so that for every
b\in B
either

(9.30)

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{j}}[b]=\phi(p_{j}),

or

(9.31)

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]=\mathbb{B}^{M}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}}[b]=\phi(p_{i}).

Suppose that there exists
b_{0}\in B
satisfying (
9.30
). Applying (
9.23
) to all
b^{\prime}\in B
with
(b_{0}-b^{\prime},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}
, we get

|B\cap\Pi(b_{0},p_{k}^{2})|\geq p_{j}^{2}>p_{i}p_{j},

which contradicts Lemma
4.3
.

Hence (
9.31
) must hold for all
b\in B
, so that
B
is a union of disjoint
N_{j}
-fibers in the
p_{i}
direction, each of cardinality
p_{i}
. On the other hand, by Claim 3 and (
9.23
), for any
b\in B
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}/p_{i}^{2}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}^{2}}[b]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}/p_{j}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b]

\displaystyle=1+\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b]

\displaystyle=1+\phi(p_{j})=p_{j}.

This implies that
p_{j}
is divisible by
p_{i}
, which is obviously false.
∎

Claim 6.

If
M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, then
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that
M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)

and
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
. Then the conclusions of Claims 1-5 apply. By Claim 5, we may find
b_{j},b_{k}
such that (
9.23
) holds with
b=b_{j}
and (
9.25
) holds with
b=b_{k}
. We fix these elements for the duration of the proof.

We claim that

(9.32)

M/p_{j}^{2},M/p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

We prove the first part of (
9.32
), the second part being identical with
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
interchanged.

As in the proof of Claim 5, we use (
9.14
) and the assumption that

M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
to conclude that either (
9.30
) or (
9.31
) holds
for every
b\in B
.
Suppose that (
9.31
) holds for
b=b_{j}
. Then
B\cap\Lambda(b_{j},D(N_{j}))
is a union of disjoint
N_{j}
-fibers in the
p_{i}
direction, and the same argument as in the proof of Claim 5
implies that
p_{j}
is divisible by
p_{i}
, which is obviously false. Thus (
9.30
) holds with
b=b_{j}
, and in particular
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
.

With (
9.32
) in place, we complete the proof as follows.
Fix
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
and
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
such that
p_{i}^{2}|a_{j}-a_{i}
. (This is possible by (
9.15
).)
Taking (
9.24
), (
9.26
) and (
9.32
) into account, we see that

(a_{i}-a_{j},M)\in\{M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}\}.

Suppose first that

(a_{i}-a_{j},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}=p_{i}^{2}
. By (
9.15
) again, we must in fact have
1,p_{i},p_{i}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
.
We deduce that
M_{i}/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}\not\in{\mathrm{Div}}_{M_{i}}(B)
, and, therefore,
one of
B\subset b*p_{j}\mathbb{Z}
or
B\subset b*p_{k}\mathbb{Z}
must hold for any fixed
b\in B
. That, however, contradicts (
9.32
).

We are therefore left with

(9.33)

(a_{i}-a_{j},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}.

We claim that in this case
\Phi_{M_{j}}\nmid A
. Indeed, otherwise
\Phi_{M_{j}}|A
and, by Lemma
9.8
, we have
\Phi_{M_{j}}|\mathcal{J}
. Notice that by (
9.32
),
\mathbb{J}^{M_{j}}_{M_{j}}[x]\in\{0,p_{j}\}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
. By Lemma
4.7
,
a_{j}
belongs to an
M_{j}
-fiber in the
p_{\nu}
direction, with either
\nu=i
or
\nu=k
. Assume first that
a_{j}
belongs to an
M_{j}
-fiber in the
p_{k}
direction. This means that for every
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(y-a_{j},M)=M/p_{k}
we have
\mathbb{J}^{M_{j}}_{M_{j}}[y]=p_{j}
, with
\mathcal{J}\cap\ell_{j}(y)
consisting of a single
M
-fiber in the
p_{j}
direction. By (
9.33
), there must be
y_{0}
with
(y_{0}-a_{j},M)=M/p_{k}
, such that
a_{i}\in\ell_{j}(y_{0})
. Let
a_{j}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}\cap\ell_{j}(y_{0})
.
Let also
a^{\prime}_{i}\in\ell_{i}(a_{i})\subset A
be the element such that
p_{i}^{2}|a^{\prime}_{i}-a^{\prime}_{j}
. We also have
p_{k}^{2}|a^{\prime}_{i}-a^{\prime}_{j}
, and, by (
9.33
),
p_{j}\nmid a^{\prime}_{i}-a^{\prime}_{j}
, Hence
(a^{\prime}_{i}-a_{j}^{\prime},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}
, which contradicts (
9.32
).

It follows that
a_{j}
must belong to an
M_{j}
-fiber in the
p_{i}
direction. By the same argument as above, for every
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(y-a_{j},M)=M/p_{i}
, we have
\mathbb{J}^{M_{j}}_{M_{j}}[y]=p_{j}
. We get
|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{k}^{2})|\geq p_{i}p_{j}
, and by Lemma
4.3
, we have

(9.34)

|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{k}^{2})|=p_{i}p_{j}.

Therefore

\displaystyle|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{k})|

\displaystyle\geq|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{k}^{2})|+|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|

\displaystyle>p_{i}p_{j},

where the latter inequality follows from the fact that
\ell_{i}(a_{i})\subset A
, as follows from Claim 1.
By Corollary
4.4
we have
\Phi_{p_{k}^{2}}|A
. Combining the latter with (
9.34
), we conclude

|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|=|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{k}^{2})|=p_{i}p_{j}>|\ell_{i}(a_{i})|=p_{i}^{2}.

This means that there must be at least one element
a\in A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})
which does not belong to
\ell_{i}(a_{i})
. The latter is not possible, because
\hbox{Div}(a,\ell_{i}(a_{i}))\subset\hbox{Div}(A)
would cause a divisor conflict with either (
9.24
) or (
9.32
). This contradiction gives
\Phi_{M_{j}}|B
.

Note that by (
9.12
)
M/p_{j}\notin\hbox{Div}(B)
, thus
B^{\prime}=\{b\mod N_{j}:\ b\in B\}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{N_{j}}
is a set (and not a multiset). By (
9.14
), we have
\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{M_{j}}|B
and therefore
\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{M_{j}}|B^{\prime}
. By Example (1) at the end of Section 2.6 applied with
M
replaced by
N_{j}
,
B^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{T}
-null with respect to the cuboid type
\mathcal{T}=(N_{j},(1,0,1),1)
. Since each individual cuboid of type
\mathcal{T}
is in fact contained in a 2-dimensional
D(M_{j})
-grid in
\mathbb{Z}_{N_{j}}
, this implies that
\Phi_{M_{j}}|(B^{\prime}\cap\Lambda)(X)
, for any such grid
\Lambda
. By Lemma
4.7
(ii),
B^{\prime}\cap\Lambda
is
N_{j}
-fibered in either the
p_{i}
or
p_{k}
direction on each such grid.
However, since
M/p_{j},M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin\hbox{Div}(B)
, we get
N_{j}/p_{k}\notin\hbox{Div}_{N_{j}}(B^{\prime})
. Hence
B^{\prime}
, and therefore
B
, is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction. As already proved in Claim 5, the latter is not allowed. The claim follows.
∎

Claim 6 proves Proposition
9.14
in the case when
M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
. From here on, we will therefore assume that

(9.35)

M/p_{j}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

Claim 7.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
and (
9.35
) holds, then
\Phi_{M_{i}/p_{j}}\Phi_{M_{i}/p_{k}}|B
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\Phi_{M_{i}/p_{j}}|A
. Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, and let
x_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(a_{i}-x_{k},M)=M/p_{k}
.
By Claim 4, we have
\mathbb{A}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[x_{k}]=0
. Furthermore, (
9.15
) and the assumption that

M/p_{j}p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
imply that

\mathbb{A}^{M_{i}/p_{j}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[x_{k}]=\mathbb{A}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[x_{k}]+\sum_{x_{k}^{\prime}:(x_{k}-x_{k}^{\prime},M)=M/p_{j}}\mathbb{A}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[x_{k}^{\prime}]=0.

Considering all
M_{i}/p_{j}
cuboids with vertices at
a_{i}
and
x_{k}
, we see that for every
x_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(a_{i}-x_{j},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}
we have

\mathbb{A}^{M_{i}/p_{j}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[x_{j}]\geq p_{i}^{2},

thus

|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|\geq p_{j}p_{i}^{2}>p_{i}p_{j},

contradicting Lemma
4.3
. Since this argument is symmetric with respect to
j
and
k
, the claim follows.
∎

Claim 8.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
and (
9.35
) holds, then
B
is
M_{i}
-fibered in both of the
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
directions, so that
for all
b\in B
we have

(9.36)

\frac{1}{\phi(p_{j})}\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b]=\frac{1}{\phi(p_{k})}\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{k}}[b]=1.

Proof.

By Claims 2 and 7,
we have
\Phi_{M_{i}}\Phi_{M_{i}/p_{k}}|B
. Therefore
B
is null with respect to all cuboids of type
(M_{i},\vec{\delta},1)
, where
\vec{\delta}=(0,1,\delta_{k})
with
\delta_{k}\in\{1,2\}
.
Suppose that for some
b_{0}\in B
we have
\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b_{0}]<\phi(p_{j})
. Fix
y_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
such that
(b_{0}-y_{j},M)=M/p_{j}
and
\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[y_{j}]=0
, and consider all cuboids as above with vertices at
b_{0}
and
y_{j}
. In order to balance these cuboids, we must have
\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[y]=1
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(b_{0}-y,M)\in\{M/p_{k},M/p_{k}^{2}\}
. But now we get
|B\cap\Pi(b_{0},p_{j}^{2})|\geq p_{k}^{2}>p_{i}p_{k}
, which contradicts Lemma
4.3
. Since this argument is symmetric in
j
and
k
, the claim follows.
∎

Claim 9.

If
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
and (
9.35
) holds, then for all
b\in B
we have
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]=\phi(p_{i})
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that there exists
b_{0}\in B
with
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b_{0}]<\phi(p_{i})
. Since
p_{k}>p_{j}
and
M/p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
, we must also have
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{k}}[b_{0}]<\phi(p_{k})
. Let
y_{i},y_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(b_{0}-y_{i},N_{j})=N_{j}/p_{i},(b_{0}-y_{k},N_{j})=N_{j}/p_{k}
and such that
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}}[y_{i}]=\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}}[y_{k}]=0
.
Recall that
\Phi_{N_{j}}|B
, and consider all
N_{j}
cuboids with vertices at
b,y_{i}
and
y_{k}
. We get that
for every
z
with
(z-b_{0},N_{j})=N_{j}/p_{j}
, we have

(9.37)

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}}[z]+\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}p_{k}}[z]\geq 1.

But by Claim 8, we also have

|B\cap\Pi(b,p_{j})|\geq p_{j}p_{k}\ \ \forall b\in B.

Applying this to all
b\in B
contributing to (
9.37
), and summing over
z
with
(z-b_{0},N_{j})=N_{j}/p_{j}
,
we get
|B|\geq p_{j}\cdot(p_{j}p_{k})>p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
, a contradiction.
∎

Claim 10.

If (
9.35
) holds, then
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

Assume, for contradiction, that (
9.35
) holds and
\Phi_{N_{i}}|A
.
By Claim 8,
B
must satisfy (
9.36
), and in particular

\mathbb{B}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}/p_{j}}[b]=\phi(p_{j})\text{ for all }b\in B.

By Claim 9,

\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]=\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}}[b]=\phi(p_{i})\text{ for all }b\in B.

As in the proof of Claim 6, these two properties imply that
p_{j}
is divisible by
p_{i}
, which is false.
∎

Claim 10 concludes the proof of Proposition
9.14
. ∎

By Proposition
9.14
, it remains to prove Proposition
9.2
under the assumption that

(9.38)

\prod_{\nu\in\{i,j,k\}}\Phi_{M/p_{\nu}}|B.

Lemma 9.15
.

Assume (F1) and (
9.38
).
Then at least one of the sets
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}
, or
\mathcal{K}
must be empty.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that (
9.12
) holds. By (
9.38
),
\Phi_{N_{i}}|B
. Since
p_{i}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
and
M/p_{j},M/p_{k}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, we must have

\frac{1}{\phi(p_{j})}\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{j}}[b],\frac{1}{\phi(p_{k})}\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{k}}[b]<1\text{ for all }b\in B,

and, in particular,
B
cannot be
N_{i}
-fibered in either the
p_{j}
or the
p_{k}
direction on any
D(N_{i})
-grid. By Lemma
4.9
, it follows that
M/p_{i}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
.

Suppose first that
\Phi_{M_{i}}|A
, so that
\Phi_{M_{i}}|\mathcal{I}
. We have
\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[x]\in\{0,p_{i}\}
for all
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
,
and
N_{i}/p_{i}\not\in{\mathrm{Div}}_{N_{i}}(A)
. By Lemma
4.7
,
\mathcal{I}
must be
M_{i}
-fibered in one of the
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
directions, on each
D(M_{i})
-grid. In particular, for a given
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, we have either

(9.39)

\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[y]=p_{i},\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(y-a_{i},M)=M/p_{j},

or

(9.40)

\mathbb{I}^{M_{i}}_{M_{i}}[y]=p_{i},\text{ for all }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(y-a_{i},M)=M/p_{k}.

Assume that there exists
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
satisfying (
9.39
), and fix such an element. It follows that
|\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|\geq p_{i}p_{j}
. By Lemma
4.3
, the last inequality holds as equality, i.e.,

(9.41)

|\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})|=p_{i}p_{j}\text{ and so }A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})\subset\mathcal{I}.

We now consider two cases.

•

If
\Phi_{p_{k}^{2}}|A
, then (
9.41
) implies
A\subset\Pi(a_{i},p_{k})
. But since
\mathcal{K}
is nonempty, we may find
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\subset\Pi(a_{i},p_{k})
. It follows that there must exist
a_{k}^{\prime}\in(a_{k}*F_{k})\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{k}^{2})
. By (
9.41
) we have
a_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
, thus contradicting (
9.11
).

•

If
\Phi_{p_{k}}|A
, then

(9.42)

|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{k})|=p_{i}p_{j}\text{ for all }a\in A.

Evidently, the latter implies that (
9.39
) must hold for all
a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
. Indeed, otherwise there would be
a_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
satisfying (
9.40
), and so
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i}^{\prime},p_{k})|\geq p_{i}p_{k}
. But now (
9.13
) implies
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i}^{\prime},p_{k})|>p_{i}p_{j}
, which contradicts (
9.42
). Hence (
9.39
) holds for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
. Consequently (
9.41
) holds for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.

Let
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
. If
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{k})
is nonempty, then (
9.41
) and (
9.42
) imply that
A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{k})
is contained in
\mathcal{I}
. In particular we would have
a_{k}\in\mathcal{I}
, thus contradicting (
9.11
). Hence
A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{k})\subset\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}
.
Lemma
9.10
(ii) implies now that
p_{i}>p_{k}
, contradicting (
9.13
).

We note that the complementary case, in which (
9.40
) holds for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
, is proved in the exact same way, interchanging
j
and
k
. The only difference between the two cases is that in the analogous subcase
\Phi_{p_{j}}|A
, we do not require the argument showing (
9.40
) holds for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.

We conclude that
\Phi_{M_{i}}\nmid A
, hence
\Phi_{N_{i}}\Phi_{M_{i}}|B
. This implies that
B
is
\mathcal{T}
-null with respect to the cuboid type
\mathcal{T}=(N_{i},\vec{\delta},T)
, where
\vec{\delta}=(0,1,1)
and
T(X)=1
. (See the first of the two examples at the end of Section
2.6
, with
M
replaced by
N_{i}
.)
Since
M/p_{i}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, we have
\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{T}}[y]\in\{0,1\}
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
. Note that
\mathcal{T}
is a 2-dimensional cuboid type, so that for every
b\in B
we either have

\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{T}}[y_{j}]=\mathbb{B}_{M}[y_{j}]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}}[y_{j}]=1\text{ for all }y_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y_{j},M)=M/p_{j},

or

\mathbb{B}^{\mathcal{T}}[y_{k}]=\mathbb{B}_{M}[y_{k}]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}}[y_{k}]=1\text{ for all }y_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y_{k},M)=M/p_{k}.

As
p_{i}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
, the former implies
M/p_{j}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, and the latter implies
M/p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, both contradicting (
9.12
).
∎

9.5.
Proof of Proposition
9.3

In this section, we will prove that
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
under the following conditions.

Assumption (F2).
We have
A\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, where
M=p_{i}^{2}p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}
is odd. Furthermore,
|A|=|B|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
,
\Phi_{M}|A
,
A
is fibered on
D(M)
-grids,
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
, and

(9.43)

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}=\emptyset.

Let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
By Lemma
9.5
(i), we have

(9.44)

a*F_{j}*F_{k}=A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2}),

and in particular

(9.45)

\{M/p_{j},M/p_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k}\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A).

We first prove Proposition
9.3
under the assumption that
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
.

Lemma 9.16
.

Assume (F2), and let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
. If
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
, then

(9.46)

A\subset\Pi(a,p_{i}).

Proof.

The assumption
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
, together with (
9.44
), implies that
|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}=|A|
.
∎

Corollary 9.17
.

Assume that (F2) holds, and that
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
. Then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
. Suppose, by contradiction, that
\mathcal{I}
is nonempty. By (
9.46
), there must exist an element
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})
. It follows from (
9.44
) that
a_{i}\in a*F_{j}*F_{k}
. But then
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
, contradicting (
9.43
).
∎

In the rest of this section, it remains to consider the following case.

Assumption (F2’).
Assume that (F2) holds, and that

(9.47)

\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}\nmid A.

Lemma 9.18
.

Assume (F2’). Then:

(i)

We have
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
. Moreover,

(9.48)

A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})=A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})\ \ \forall a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K},

(9.49)

|A\cap\Pi(x^{\prime},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}\ \ \forall x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}.

(ii)

Let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
For every
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus A
with
(a-x,M)=M/p_{i}
, we have

(9.50)

A_{x}\subset\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2}).

Proof.

Since
|A|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
, exactly one of
\Phi_{p_{i}}
and
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}
must divide
A
.
By (
9.47
), we must in fact have
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
.
Let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
Then

(9.51)

p_{j}p_{k}\geq|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})|\geq|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})|=p_{j}p_{k},

by (
9.44
). Hence the above must hold with equality, which proves (
9.48
). It also follows that for any
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, we must have
|A\cap\Pi(x^{\prime},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
, so that (
9.49
) holds. This proves (i).

Next, let
a,x
be as in (ii). By (
9.51
) again, we have

(9.52)

A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})\subset\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2}).

Let
b\in B
. Applying Corollary
2.9
(i) to
A_{x,b}
, from (
2.5
) we get
A_{x,b}\subset\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})\cup\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})
. Now (
9.50
) follows directly from (
9.52
).
∎

Lemma 9.19
.

Assume (F2’). Then:

(i)

For every
b\in B
, and for every
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(b-y,M)=M/p_{i}
, we have

(9.53)

\mathbb{B}_{M}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{k}}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}[y]=1.

(ii)

For all
d
with
p_{i}^{2}|d|(M/p_{j}p_{k})
, we have
\Phi_{d}|B
. Additionally,

(9.54)

\{M/p_{i},M/p_{i}p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)\neq\emptyset.

Proof.

Fix
b\in B
, and write
N_{jk}=M/p_{j}p_{k}
for short.
Let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
, and let
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus A
with
(a-x,M)=M/p_{i}
.

By (
2.1
), (
9.44
), and (
9.50
), we have

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i})}\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}}[x|\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})]\,\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}}[b]+\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i}p_{j})}\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}}[x|\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})]\,\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}}[b]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i}p_{k})}\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}p_{k}}[x|\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})]\,\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{k}}[b]+\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i}p_{j}p_{k})}\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}}[x|\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})]\,\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}}[b]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i})}(\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}}[b]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}}[b]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{k}}[b]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}}[b])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(p_{i})}\sum_{y:(y-b,M)=M/p_{i}}\mathbb{B}_{N_{jk}}^{N_{jk}}[y].

On the other hand, by (
9.45
) and divisor exclusion, any
N_{jk}
-grid may contain at most one element of
B
, so that
\mathbb{B}_{N_{jk}}^{N_{jk}}[y]\leq 1
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
. Therefore

\mathbb{B}_{N_{jk}}^{N_{jk}}[y]=1\text{ for all }y\text{ such that }(y-b,M)=M/p_{i},

which is (
9.53
). We also note that the first equation in the above calculation implies (
9.54
).

Finally, let
d\neq p_{i}^{2}
and
p_{i}^{2}|d|(M/p_{j}p_{k})
, and consider any
d
-cuboid with one vertex at
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
. By (
9.44
), we have
\mathbb{A}^{d}_{d}[a]\geq p_{j}p_{k}
. However, we also have
|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
by the third claim in the lemma, so that
\mathbb{A}^{d}_{d^{\prime}}[a]=0
for all
d^{\prime}<d
with
D(d)|d^{\prime}
. It follows that the cuboid cannot be balanced. Therefore
\Phi_{d}\nmid A
, which proves (ii).
∎

Lemma 9.20
.

Assume (F2’). Then
\mathcal{I}\cap(\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose that the conclusion fails. Without loss of generality, we may assume that

\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
. Taking into account that
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
, we have from (
9.45
)

M/p_{i},M/p_{j},M/p_{k},M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A)

so that (
9.54
) reduces to
\{M/p_{i}p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)\neq\emptyset
, and (
9.53
) to

\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}[y]=1\text{ for all }y\text{ with }(y-b,M)=M/p_{i}.

This means we must have

(9.55)

p_{i}<p_{j},

otherwise one must introduce
M/p_{i}
or
M/p_{i}p_{k}
as differences in
B
.

We now repeat the same procedure with
i
and
j
interchanged. Let
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{K}
. We note that
A\cap\Lambda(a^{\prime},D(M))
cannot be
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction, since that would contradict (
9.43
).
It follows from Lemma
9.5
(i) that
a^{\prime}*F_{i}*F_{k}\subset A
, and, together with Lemma
4.3
, this implies that

(9.56)

a^{\prime}*F_{i}*F_{k}=A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j}^{2}).

Let
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus A
satisfy
(a^{\prime}-x^{\prime},M)=M/p_{j}
. As in the proof of Lemma
9.18
, by Corollary
2.9
(i)
we have

A_{x^{\prime}}\subset\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j}^{2})\cup\Pi(x^{\prime},p_{j}^{2}).

Assume, by contradiction, that there exists a
b\in B
such that
A_{x^{\prime},b}\subset\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j}^{2})
. Repeating the proof of Lemma
9.19
with that
b
, we get the analogues of (
9.53
) and
(
9.54
) with
i
and
j
interchanged.
The same argument as in the proof of (
9.55
) shows then that
p_{j}<p_{i}
, a contradiction.

It follows that
A_{x^{\prime}}\cap\Pi(x^{\prime},p_{j}^{2})\neq\emptyset
, and, in particular,

|A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j})|>p_{i}p_{k}
. By Corollary
4.4
,

A\subset\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j})
. In particular,

\mathcal{J}\subset\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j})
, with each fiber in
\mathcal{J}
containing a point in
A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{j}^{2})
.
But by (
9.56
), any such point would belong to
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
, contradicting (
9.43
).
∎

Lemma 9.21
.

Assume (F2’). If
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, then
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.8
, it suffices to prove that
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid\mathcal{I}
. Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
. By Corollary
9.20
, we have
a_{i}\not\in\mathcal{J}
and
a_{i}\not\in\mathcal{K}
, so that there must exist
x_{j},x_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus A
with
(a_{i}-x_{j},N_{i})=N_{i}/p_{j},(a_{i}-x_{k},N_{i})=N_{i}/p_{k}
and such that
\mathbb{I}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[x_{j}]=\mathbb{I}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[x_{k}]=0
.

Consider the
N_{i}
cuboid with one face containing vertices at
a_{i},x_{j}
and
x_{k}
, and the other face in
\Pi(a,p_{i})
, where
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
. In order for this cuboid to be balanced,
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})
must be nonempty,
and in particular
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})\neq\emptyset
. But this together with (
9.44
) contradicts
(
9.43
).
∎

Lemma 9.22
.

Assume (F2’). If
p_{i}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
, then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Assume by contradiction that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
9.21
,
\Phi_{N_{i}}\nmid A
, hence
\Phi_{N_{i}}|B
. By assumption we have
M/p_{i}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
, so that
\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[y]\in\{0,1\}
for all
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
. By (
9.47
),
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|B
.
Hence
\Phi_{p_{i}}\nmid B
, and in particular
B
cannot be
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction. It follows that there must exist
b_{0}\in B
and
y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(b_{0}-y,N_{i})=N_{i}/p_{i}
and
\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}}[y]=0
.

In order to simplify notation, we shall denote
\beta_{\nu}=\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{\nu}}[b_{0}]
for
\nu\in\{j,k\}
. By (
9.53
) we must have

(9.57)

\beta_{j}+\beta_{k}+1\leq|B\cap\Lambda(b_{0},D(M))|\leq p_{i}

thus

(9.58)

\beta_{\nu}\leq(p_{i}-1)/2\text{ for some }\nu\in\{j,k\}.

In addition, considering all
N_{i}
cuboids with vertices at
b_{0}
and
y
such that the vertices at distance
N_{i}/p_{j}
and
N_{i}/p_{k}
from
b_{0}
do not belong to
B
, we see that

\mathbb{B}^{N_{i}}_{N_{i}/p_{j}p_{k}}[y]\geq(p_{j}-\beta_{j}-1)(p_{k}-\beta_{k}-1).

Now, if
\beta_{j}=1
then
\beta_{k}\leq p_{i}-2
and so

\displaystyle(p_{j}-\beta_{j}-1)(p_{k}-\beta_{k}-1)

\displaystyle\geq(p_{j}-2)(p_{k}-p_{i}+1)

\displaystyle\geq(p_{k}-p_{i}+1)p_{i}

\displaystyle>p_{i}

which contradicts (
9.53
). We may therefore assume
\beta_{\nu}\geq 2
for
\nu=j,k
. In this case, however, assuming (
9.58
) for
\nu=k
, applying (
9.57
) and the fact that
p_{j}-p_{i}\geq 2
, we have

\displaystyle(p_{j}-\beta_{j}-1)(p_{k}-\beta_{k}-1)

\displaystyle=(p_{j}-p_{i}+p_{i}-\beta_{j}-1)(p_{k}-\beta_{k}-1)

\displaystyle\geq(p_{j}-p_{i}+\beta_{k})(p_{k}-\beta_{k}-1)

\displaystyle\geq 4(p_{i}-(p_{i}-1)/2-1)

\displaystyle=2p_{i}-2

The latter exceeds
p_{i}
whenever
p_{i}>2
. Since
M
is odd, again we get a contradiction to (
9.53
) and the lemma follows.
∎

Lemma
9.22
proves Proposition
9.3
, assuming that (F2’) holds and that
p_{i}
is the smallest prime. From now on, we will therefore assume that

(9.59)

p_{i}>\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}.

The rest of the proof will be split into the following cases:

•

Assume (F2’), (
9.59
), and

\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
. This case is addressed in Lemma
9.23
and Corollary
9.24
.

•

Assume (F2’), (
9.59
), and (interchanging
j
and
k
if necessary)

\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
,
\Phi_{N_{k}}\nmid B
.
This case is addressed in Lemmas
9.27
,
9.28
,
and
9.29
.

•

Assume (F2’), (
9.59
), and

\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
. This case is addressed in Lemma
9.30
, Corollary
9.31
, and Lemma
9.32
.

Lemma 9.23
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
).
If
p_{k}<p_{i}<p_{j}
and
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
, then
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{J}
.

Proof.

We first claim that
\mathcal{K}
is
N_{k}
-fibered on each
D(N_{k})
-grid in one of the
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
directions.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{K}
were not
N_{k}
-fibered on some
D(N_{k})
-grid, then it would follow from Lemma
9.12
that
\{D(M)|m|M\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A)
; however, that is not compatible with (
9.54
).
Furthermore, by Lemma
9.20
and Lemma
9.11
(i),

\mathcal{K}
cannot be
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction on any
D(N_{k})
-grid. This proves the claim.

Recall from Lemma
9.18
(i) that for any
a_{0}\in A
,

(9.60)

|A\cap\Pi(a_{0},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}.

If
\mathcal{K}
is
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction on some
D(N_{k})
-grid
\Lambda_{j}
,
then for every
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda_{j}
we have

(9.61)

\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{j}}[a_{k}]=p_{k}\phi(p_{j}),

so that
a_{k}\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
. By (
9.60
),

A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})=a_{k}*F_{j}*F_{k}
is fibered in both directions, and in particular
\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})
contains no elements of
A
outside of
\Lambda_{j}
.

Assume now that there exists
a^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathcal{K}
is
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction on
\Lambda_{k}:=\Lambda(a^{\prime}_{k},D(N_{k}))
.
Then

(9.62)

\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[a^{\prime}]=p_{k}\phi(p_{k})\text{ for all }a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i}),

so that

(9.63)

\mathbb{A}_{M}[a^{\prime}]+\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{k}}[a^{\prime}]+\mathbb{A}_{M/p_{k}^{2}}[a^{\prime}]=p_{k}^{2}\text{ for all }a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i}).

Fix
a^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfying (
9.62
) and (
9.63
). We first claim that

(9.64)

\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i})=\emptyset.

Indeed, suppose that (
9.64
) fails, and let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i}^{2})
. Since
\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda_{k}
is
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction, by Lemma
9.20

we must have
a_{i}\notin\Lambda_{k}
, so that
a_{i}
must be at distance
M/p_{j}^{2}
from the fiber chain in the
p_{k}
direction rooted at
a^{\prime}_{k}
. We can now extend (
9.45
) to

(9.65)

M/p_{j},M/p_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k},M/p_{j}^{2},M/p_{k}^{2},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A),

where all the differences that do not appear in (
9.45
), come from the interaction between
a_{i}
and
\ell_{k}(a^{\prime}_{k})
.

By (
9.65
), we see that for every
b\in B

\displaystyle|B\cap\Pi(b,p_{i}^{2})|

\displaystyle\leq 1+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}^{2}}[b]

\displaystyle\leq p_{k}.

But, since
\mathbb{Z}_{M}
has only
p_{i}^{2}
residue classes modulo
p_{i}^{2}
, it follows that

p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}=|B|\leq p_{i}^{2}p_{k},

so that
p_{j}\leq p_{i}
, contradicting the assumption that (
9.64
) fails.

It, therefore, follows that
A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}
. By (
9.60
) and Lemma
9.10
(i), we have
A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{K}
. But since all
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a^{\prime}_{k},p_{i})
satisfy (
9.62
) and (
9.63
), we get

p_{j}p_{k}=cp_{k}^{2}

for some positive integer
c
. The latter implies
p_{k}
divides
p_{j}
, which is not allowed. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 9.24
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
). Assume further that

\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
and that
p_{k}<p_{j}
.
Then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, and
A
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction.

Proof.

By (
9.59
), we have
p_{k}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
.
We first apply Corollary
9.13
, with
j
and
k
interchanged. Since
\Phi_{N_{j}}|A
and
p_{j}\neq\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
,
we get that
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{K}
. However, by Lemma
9.20
we have
\mathcal{I}\cap\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
, so that we must in fact have
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{K}
.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\mathcal{I}
is nonempty. We first prove that this implies

(9.66)

p_{i}<p_{j}.

Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
. Observe that
\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
cannot contain any elements
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{J}
, since any such element would be associated with a grid
a^{\prime}*F_{j}*F_{k}\subset A
. This together with Lemma
9.20
would imply

|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})|\geq|a_{i}*F_{i}|+|a^{\prime}*F_{j}*F_{k}|>p_{j}p_{k},

contradicting (
9.60
). We get
\mathcal{J}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})=\emptyset
.
Hence
A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{I}\cup\mathcal{K}
, and (
9.66
) follows from Lemma
9.10
(ii).

Applying Lemma
9.23
, we see that
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{J}
. This, together with the first part of the proof, implies
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
. Hence any element
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
is associated with a grid
a_{k}*F_{j}*F_{k}\subset A
, and as shown above, such grids cannot intersect
\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
. Therefore
A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
must be contained in
\mathcal{I}
. That, however, is clearly false since
p_{j}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})|
cannot be a multiple of
p_{i}
. This contradiction concludes the proof.
∎

Before we move on to the next two cases, we need two lemmas on the fibering properties of
B
.

Lemma 9.25
.

Assume (F2). If
\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
, then
B
is
N_{k}
-fibered on each
D(N_{k})
-grid, either in the
p_{k}
direction or in the
p_{i}
direction. The same is true with
j
and
k
interchanged.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
. By (
9.45
), we have
\mathbb{B}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{j}}[b]=0
and
\mathbb{B}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}}[b]=1
for all
b\in B
. Since
M
is odd, the lemma follows from Lemma
4.9
.
∎

Lemma 9.26
.

Assume (F2’). If
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
and
B
is
N_{i}
-fibered on a
D(N_{i})
-grid, then it must be fibered in the
p_{i}
direction on that grid.

Proof.

We argue by contradiction. Let
\Lambda_{0}:=\Lambda(b,D(N_{i}))
for some
b\in B
, and assume that
B\cap\Lambda_{0}
is
N_{i}
-fibered in one of the other directions, say
p_{j}
. Let also
\Lambda:=\Lambda(b,D(M))
.
By (
9.53
), we have

(9.67)

|B\cap\Lambda|=p_{i}.

On the other hand, the
N_{i}
-fibering assumption means that

B\cap\Lambda_{0}
can be divided into mutually disjoint
N_{i}
-fibers in the
p_{j}
direction, each one of cardinality
p_{j}
, and each one either entirely contained in
\Lambda
or disjoint from it. This implies that
p_{j}
divides
|B\cap\Lambda|
. That, however, contradicts (
9.67
).
∎

Next, we consider the case
\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A,\Phi_{N_{k}}\nmid B
. This case will be split further, according to whether
p_{j}
or
p_{k}
is the smallest prime.

Lemma 9.27
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
).
Assume further that
\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
,
\Phi_{N_{k}}\nmid B
, and

p_{j}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
. Then
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
. In addition, if
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, then:

•

B
is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction,

•

|A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
for any
a^{\prime}\in A
,

•

p_{i}<p_{k}
.

Proof.

We first note that the claim
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
follows from Corollary
9.13
and Lemma
9.20
, while the second bullet point follows from Lemma
9.18
(i).
Next, let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
. Then
\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
is nonempty and, by Lemma
9.20
, disjoint from
\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{J}
. By Lemma
9.10
(ii) with
j
and
k
interchanged, it follows that
p_{i}<p_{k}
.

It remains to prove the first point. We have
\Phi_{N_{j}}|B
, hence by Lemma
9.25
,
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered on each
D(N_{j})
-grid in one of the
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
directions.
Since
p_{j}<p_{i}
, it follows that
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]<\phi(p_{i})
for all
b\in B
, hence
B
must be
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction.
∎

Lemma 9.28
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
). Assume further that

\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
,
\Phi_{N_{k}}\nmid B
, and
p_{j}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
,
Then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, and
A
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
. By Lemma
9.21
,
\Phi_{N_{i}}|B
.

Assume first that
N_{i}/p_{i}\notin{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
. By Lemma
4.9
,

B
is fibered on
D(N_{i})
-grids in one of the
p_{j}
and
p_{k}
direction.
That, however, contradicts Lemma
9.26
.

Suppose now that
b,b^{\prime}\in B
with
(b-b^{\prime},N_{i})=N_{i}/p_{i}
. By Lemma
9.27
,
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction. This together with
(
9.53
) implies that
|B\cap\Pi(b,p_{k})|>p_{i}p_{j}
. By Corollary
4.4
, we have
\Phi_{p_{k}^{2}}|B
, hence
\Phi_{p_{k}}|A
. The latter, in turn, implies

(9.68)

|A\cap\Pi(a^{\prime},p_{k})|=p_{i}p_{j}\ \ \forall a^{\prime}\in A.

On the other hand, let
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
with
a*F_{j}*F_{k}\subset A
as provided by (
9.44
). Then

|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{k})|\geq p_{j}p_{k}>p_{i}p_{j},

where at the last step we used Lemma
9.27
again.
This contradicts (
9.68
).

This proves that
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
. By the second claim in Lemma
9.27
, we have
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, hence
A
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction.
∎

Lemma 9.29
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
). Assume further that

\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
,
\Phi_{N_{k}}\nmid B
, and
p_{k}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
.
Then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
and
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
. Consequently,
A
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction.

Proof.

The proof splits between two cases.

Case 1:

p_{k}<p_{i}<p_{j}
.
In this case, by Lemma
9.23
we have
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
.
Assume, by contradiction, that
\mathcal{I}
is nonempty, and let

a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.
By Lemma
9.18
(i), we have
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
. By Lemma
9.10
(ii) with
j
and
k
interchanged,
it follows that
p_{k}>p_{i}
, contradicting our assumption. Therefore
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

Case 2:

p_{k}<p_{j}<p_{i}
.
We first note that if
\mathcal{I}
is nonempty, then
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b]<\phi(p_{i})
for all
b\in B
. Since
\Phi_{N_{j}}|B
, by Lemma
9.25

(9.69)

B\text{ must be }N_{j}\text{-fibered in the }p_{j}\text{ direction}.

Next, we follow the first part of the proof of Lemma
9.23
.
(This part does not use the Lemma
9.23
assumption that
p_{i}<p_{j}
.) By the same argument as there,
\mathcal{K}
must be
N_{k}
-fibered on every
D(N_{k})
-grid in either the
p_{j}
or the
p_{k}
direction, and for every
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
we have one of the following:

•

(
9.61
) holds, hence
a_{k}\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
and
a_{k}*F_{j}*F_{k}=A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})
,

•

(
9.62
) and (
9.63
) hold for all
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda(a_{k},D(N_{k}))
.

If (
9.61
) holds for all
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}
, then
\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{J}
, and it follows by the same argument as in Case 1 that
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

We now prove that the second case is impossible. Indeed,
assume by contradiction that there exists
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
such that (
9.62
) and (
9.63
) hold for all
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Lambda(a_{k},D(N_{k}))
. We first claim that

(9.70)

\mathcal{I}\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})=\emptyset.

Indeed, if (
9.70
) fails, we continue as in the proof of Lemma
9.23
and find an element
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
at distance
M/p_{j}^{2}
from the fiber chain through
a_{k}
. But (
9.69
) implies
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, which is a contradiction. Hence (
9.70
) holds.

By (
9.49
), we have
|A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
.
It follows from (
9.70
) and Lemma
9.10
(i) that
A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{K}
. As in the proof of Lemma
9.23
, all elements of
A
in
\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})
must satisfy (
9.62
). Hence

p_{j}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})|=cp_{k}^{2},

for some positive integer
c
, so that
p_{k}
divides
p_{j}
, a contradiction. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We now address the case in which
\Phi_{N_{\nu}}|B
for
\nu\in\{j,k\}
.

Lemma 9.30
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
). Assume further that

B
is
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction,
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, and that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
. Then for all
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
we have
\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})\neq\emptyset
. Replacing
a_{i}
by a different element of
a_{i}*F_{i}
if necessary, we may further assume that
p_{i}^{2}|a_{i}-a_{k}
for some
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})

Moreover, it follows from the fibering assumption on
B
that the pair
(A,B)
has a (1,2)-cofibered structure in the
p_{k}
direction, with the cofiber in
A
rooted at
a_{k}
.

Suppose that
(a_{i}-a_{k},M)=M/p_{j}p_{k}^{2}
. Applying Lemma
2.11
, we could then shift the cofiber
a_{k}*F_{k}
in the
p_{k}
direction, obtaining a new T2-equivalent tiling
A^{\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
in which the shifted cofiber
a^{\prime}_{k}*F_{k}
satisfies

(a_{i}-a_{k},M)=M/p_{j}
.
We claim that
A^{\prime}
contains a
p_{j}
extended corner structure. Indeed, by Lemma
9.20
we have

\Lambda(a_{i},D(M))\cap(\mathcal{J}\cup\mathcal{K})=\emptyset
. Hence
A\cap(a_{i}*F_{i}*F_{k})
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction but not in the
p_{k}
direction, and
A\cap(a^{\prime}_{k}*F_{i}*F_{k})
must be empty, so that
A^{\prime}\cap(a^{\prime}_{k}*F_{i}*F_{k})=a^{\prime}_{k}*F_{k}
.
This proves the claim.
However, Theorem
8.1
now implies that
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
,
contradicting (F2’).

Since
M/p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
by the fibering assumption, we are now left with
(a_{i}-a_{k},M)=M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}
. But then, by the same fiber-shifting argument as above, we get a T2-equivalent tiling
A^{\prime\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, where
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(A^{\prime\prime})
. This contradicts the assumption that
M/p_{j}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
.
∎

Corollary 9.31
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
).
If
B
is
N_{\nu}
-fibered in the
p_{\nu}
direction for both
\nu=j
and
\nu=k
, then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

By the fibering assumption,

M/p_{j}^{2},M/p_{k}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, and let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{I}
.
It follows from Lemma
9.30
that
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{K}
are both disjoint from
\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})
.
Therefore
A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{I}
, and, in particular,
p_{i}
divides
|A\cap\Pi(a_{i},p_{i})|
. But this contradicts
(
9.49
).
∎

Lemma 9.32
.

Assume (F2’) and (
9.59
). Assume further that

\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
. Then
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
p_{k}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
.
By Lemma
9.25
,
B
is
N_{k}
-fibered on
D(N_{k})
-grids in one of the
p_{i}
and
p_{k}
directions. However,
B
cannot be
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction on any
D(N_{k})
-grid, since the assumptions that
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
and
p_{k}<p_{i}
imply that
\mathbb{B}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{i}}[b]<\phi(p_{i})
for all
b\in B
.
Hence

(9.71)

B\text{ must be }N_{k}\text{-fibered in the }p_{k}\text{ direction}.

By the same argument as above, if
p_{j}<p_{i}
, then
B
must also be
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction, and an application of Corollary
9.31
concludes the proof.

It remains to consider the case when

p_{k}<p_{i}<p_{j}.

We claim that in this case,

(9.72)

M/p_{i}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B).

Indeed, since
\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
, the failure of (
9.72
) would imply that
N_{i}/p_{k}\not\in{\mathrm{Div}}_{N_{i}}(B)
. Since
\mathcal{I}\neq\emptyset
, Lemma
9.21
implies
\Phi_{N_{i}}|B
.
It would then follow from Lemma
4.9
that
B
is
N_{i}
-fibered on
D(N_{i})
-grids. By
Lemma
9.26
,
B
can only be
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction.
In particular,
\Phi_{p_{i}}|B
, contradicting Lemma
9.18
(i).

Let
b_{1},b_{2}\in B
with

(9.73)

(b_{1}-b_{2},M)=M/p_{i}p_{k}.

By (
9.53
), one may find
b_{3},\ldots,b_{p_{i}}\in B
satisfying
(b_{\nu}-b_{\nu^{\prime}},M)\in\{M/p_{i}p_{k},M/p_{i}p_{j},M/p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}\}
for all
\nu\in\{1,\dots,p_{i}\}
with
\nu\neq\nu^{\prime}
.

By Lemma
9.25
,
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered on each
D(N_{j})
-grid in one of the
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
directions.
On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle p_{i}

\displaystyle=|B\cap\Lambda(b_{1},D(M))|\geq\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b_{1}]+\mathbb{B}^{M}_{M/p_{i}p_{k}}[b_{1}]

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b_{1}]+1,

where the first equality follows from (
9.45
) and from (
9.53
) applied to
b_{2},\dots,b_{p_{i}}
, and the last inequality follows from
(
9.73
).
In particular,
\mathbb{B}^{N_{j}}_{N_{j}/p_{i}}[b_{1}]<\phi(p_{i})
, so that
B
cannot be
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction on the grid
\Lambda:=\Lambda(b_{1},D(N_{j}))
.
It follows that
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction on
\Lambda
.

Taking also (
9.71
) into account, we see that

(9.74)

|B\cap\Pi(b_{\nu},p_{i}^{2})|\geq p_{j}p_{k}\text{ for all }\nu\in\{1,2,\ldots,p_{i}\}.

Since
|B|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
, (
9.74
) must in fact hold with equality for each
\nu
, and

(9.75)

B\subset\Pi(b_{1},p_{i}).

Now, let
b\in B
be arbitrary. We claim that

(9.76)

M/p_{i}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B\cap\Lambda(b,D(N_{i})))

To prove this, we start by arguing as in the proof of (
9.72
) that if (
9.76
) fails, then
B
is
N_{i}
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction on
\Lambda(b,D(N_{i}))
. However, if that were the case, then we would have
M/p_{i}^{2}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, contradicting (
9.75
).

We further note that by (
9.75
),
B\cap\Lambda(b,D(N_{i}))=B\cap\Lambda(b,D(M))
, so that in fact we have

M/p_{i}p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B\cap\Lambda(b,D(M))\ \ \forall b\in B.

With this in place, we repeat the argument starting with (
9.73
) to prove that
B
is
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction on all
D(N_{j})
-grids.

It follows that
B
is
N_{\nu}
-fibered in the
p_{\nu}
direction for both
\nu=j
and
\nu=k
.
By Corollary
9.31
, we have

\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
as claimed.
∎

9.6.
Proof of Proposition
9.4

In this section, we are working under the following assumption.

Assumption (F3):
We have
A\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
, where
M=p_{i}^{2}p_{j}^{2}p_{k}^{2}
is odd. Furthermore,
|A|=|B|=p_{i}p_{j}p_{k}
,
\Phi_{M}|A
,
A
is fibered on
D(M)
-grids,
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
, and

(9.77)

\text{the sets }\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}\text{ and }\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}\text{ are nonempty}.

The proof below works regardless of whether
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{K}
are disjoint or not. If
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}\neq\emptyset
, then
(since
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
) any element
a\in\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
must satisfy the conditions of Lemma
9.5
(i), so that

A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i}^{2})=a*F_{j}*F_{k}.

It follows that the set
\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction, and

\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction.

We begin with the case when at least one of
\Phi_{M_{j}}
and
\Phi_{M_{k}}
divides
A
.

Lemma 9.33
.

Assume (F3), and that
\Phi_{M_{k}}|A
. Then

(9.78)

\Phi_{p_{j}^{2}}|A.

Furthermore,
\mathcal{K}
is
M_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction, so that for every
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
we have

(9.79)

\mathbb{K}^{M_{k}}_{M_{k}/p_{j}}[a_{k}]=p_{k}\cdot\phi(p_{j}).

and

(9.80)

A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})\subset\Lambda(a_{k},p_{i}^{2}p_{j}).

The same holds with
p_{k}
and
p_{j}
interchanged.

Proof.

Notice first that the fibering statement holds trivially for all
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\cap\mathcal{J}
.

Let now
\mathcal{L}:=\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}
, with the corresponding
N
-boxes
\mathbb{L}^{N}
for
N|M
.
The assumption
\Phi_{M_{k}}|A
implies, by (
9.3
), that
\Phi_{M_{k}}|\mathcal{L}
. By Lemma
4.7
,
\mathcal{L}
mod
M_{k}
is a disjoint union of
M_{k}
-fibers in the
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
direction. Hence, any element

a_{0}\in\mathcal{L}
which does not belong to an
M_{k}
-fiber in the
p_{j}
direction must satisfy

(9.81)

\mathbb{L}^{M_{k}}_{M_{k}/p_{i}}[a_{0}]=p_{k}\cdot\phi(p_{i})

and
|A\cap\Pi(a_{0},p_{j}^{2})|\geq p_{i}p_{k}
. By Lemma
4.3
,

|A\cap\Pi(a_{0},p_{j}^{2})|=p_{i}p_{k},

in particular

(9.82)

A\cap\Pi(a_{0},p_{j}^{2})\subset\mathcal{L}\text{ and }\mathcal{J}\cap\Pi(a_{0},p_{j}^{2})=\emptyset.

We first prove (
9.78
). Indeed, assume for contradiction that
\Phi_{p_{j}}|A
. Then

(9.83)

|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{j})|=p_{i}p_{k}\text{ for all }a\in A.

Let
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}
, and consider the plane system
\Pi(a_{j},p_{j})
. These planes cannot contain any elements
a\in\mathcal{L}
satisfying (
9.81
), since any such element would belong to an
M_{k}
-fiber in the
p_{i}
direction in
\mathcal{L}
, of cardinality
p_{i}p_{k}
and contained in
\Pi(a_{j},p_{j})
, and this would leave no room for the additional element
a_{j}\not\in\mathcal{L}
.

Thus every element of
A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{j})
must either belong to
\mathcal{J}
, or else it must be an element of
\mathcal{L}

belonging to an
M_{k}
-fiber in the
p_{j}
direction in
\mathcal{L}
, of cardinality
p_{j}p_{k}
. Since the two sets are disjoint,
(
9.83
) implies that

p_{i}p_{k}=|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{j})|=c_{1}p_{j}+c_{2}p_{j}p_{k},

where
c_{1},c_{2}
are nonnegative integers.
But then
p_{j}
divides either
p_{i}
or
p_{k}
, a contradiction.

Therefore
\Phi_{p_{j}^{2}}|A
. Suppose now that there actually exists an element
a_{0}\in\mathcal{L}
such that (
9.81
) holds. Then
A\subset\Pi(a_{0},p_{j})
. Since
\mathcal{J}
is nonempty, it follows that
\mathcal{J}
must intersect
\Pi(a_{0},p_{j}^{2})
, contradicting (
9.82
). This proves the fibering conclusion of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 9.34
.

Assume (F3).
The following holds true:

(i)

If
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}\Phi_{M_{k}}|A
, then
A\subset\Pi(a,p_{i})
for any
a\in A
(hence
A
is contained in a coset of
p_{i}\mathbb{Z}_{M}
).

(ii)

If
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
, then
|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
for all
a\in A
.
Moreover, for every
a\in A
we have either
A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})\subset\mathcal{J}
or
A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})\subset\mathcal{K}
.

Proof.

For part (i), by Lemma
9.33
every
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
satisfies (
9.79
). By Lemma
4.3
,

(9.84)

|A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})|=p_{j}p_{k}\text{, hence }A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})\subset\mathcal{K}.

If
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
, then
A\subset\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})
. This proves the first part of the lemma.

For part (ii), assume that
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
. Then
|A\cap\Pi(a,p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
for all
a\in A
.
The second part follows from Lemma
9.10
(i) with
\alpha_{i}=1
.
∎

Lemma 9.35
.

Assume (F3) and

(9.85)

\Phi_{p_{i}}\Phi_{M_{k}}|A.

Then
\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\Phi_{N_{j}}|A
. By (
9.77
) and Corollary
9.13
, we must have
p_{j}=\min_{\nu}p_{\nu}
. We first claim that

(9.86)

\hbox{if }\Phi_{N_{j}}|A\hbox{, then }\mathcal{J}\hbox{ must be }N_{j}\hbox{-fibered on }D(N_{j})\hbox{-grids.}

Indeed, suppose that (
9.86
) fails. By Lemma
9.12
, we have

(9.87)

\{D(M)|m|M\}\subset{\mathrm{Div}}(A).

Applying Corollary
9.13
and (
9.77
) again in the
p_{k}
direction, we get
\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
.
By (
9.87
) and Lemma
9.11
(ii),
B
is
N_{k}
-fibered in the
p_{k}
direction, implying a (1,2)-cofibered structure for
(A,B)
with all fibers in
\mathcal{K}
as cofibers.

Fix
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
, and recall that it must satisfy (
9.79
). This produces a family of
M
-fibers in the
p_{k}
direction, all contained in
A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})
.
Using Lemma
2.11
to shift and align these fibers if necessary, we get a T2-equivalent set
A^{\prime}
such that

A^{\prime}\oplus B=\mathbb{Z}_{M}
and
a_{k}*F_{j}*F_{k}\subset A^{\prime}
.
By T2-equivalence,
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A^{\prime}
, and by Lemma
4.3
,

(9.88)

a_{k}*F_{j}*F_{k}=A^{\prime}\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2}).

Since
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
,
a_{k}
cannot belong to an
M
-fiber in the
p_{i}
direction, and in particular there exists an
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\setminus A
with
(a_{k}-x,M)=M/p_{i}
. We have
A^{\prime}_{x}\subset\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})\cup\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})
. However, if
A^{\prime}_{x}\cap\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})\neq\emptyset
, then
|A^{\prime}\cap\Pi(x,p_{i}^{2})|>0
. By Corollary
4.4
and
(
9.84
), we have
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A^{\prime}
, which contradicts the fact that
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A^{\prime}
.

Thus
A^{\prime}_{x}\subset\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})
, and by (
9.88
), for any
b\in B
we have

1=\sum_{m\in\{1,p_{j},p_{k},p_{j}p_{k}\}}\frac{1}{\phi(mp_{i})}\mathbb{A}^{\prime}_{M/mp_{i}}[x|\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})]\mathbb{B}_{M/mp_{i}}[b].

Hence
\{D(M)|m|M\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
is nontrivial, contradicting (
9.87
). This proves (
9.86
).

By (
9.77
), we may find
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
. It follows from Lemma
9.11
(i) that
A\cap\Lambda(a_{j},D(N_{j}))
can only be

N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction. Recall from Lemma
9.34
that
|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
and
A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
. But the fibering in
\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
implies that

p_{j}p_{k}=c\cdot p_{j}^{2},

thus
p_{j}
must divide
p_{k}
, which is clearly false. The lemma follows.
∎

Lemma 9.36
.

Assume (F3) and (
9.85
).
Then
\Phi_{M_{k}/p_{i}}|A
.

Proof.

Denote
N=M_{k}/p_{i}
, and write

A(X)=\mathcal{J}(X)-(\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K})(X)+\mathcal{K}(X).

By Lemma
9.5
(i) and (
9.2
),
\Phi_{N}
divides both
\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}\cap\mathcal{K}
.
Hence it suffices to prove that
\Phi_{N}|\mathcal{K}
.

Let
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
. By Lemma
9.33
, we have
\mathbb{K}^{M_{k}}_{M_{k}}[a_{k}]=\mathbb{K}^{M_{k}}_{M_{k}}[x_{j}]=p_{k}
for all
x_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with
(a_{k}-x_{j},M)=M/p_{j}
. In particular,
|\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})|=p_{j}p_{k}
, and, since
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
, there are no other elements of
A
in
\Pi(a_{k},p_{i})
.
It follows that, for any
x_{j}
as above,

(9.89)

\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[a_{k}]=\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[x_{j}]=p_{k}

We need to prove that
\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[\Delta]=0
for every
N
-cuboid
\Delta
. It suffices to check this under the assumption that at least one vertex of
\Delta
belongs to
\mathcal{K}
, so that two of its vertices are at points
a_{k}
and
x_{j}
as above. The other two vertices are at
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
with

(a_{k}-x,N)=(x_{j}-x^{\prime},N)=N/p_{i}\text{ and }(x-x^{\prime},N)=N/p_{j}.

By (
9.89
), the cuboid face containing
a_{k}
and
x_{j}
is balanced. Consider now the face containing
x
and
x^{\prime}
. By Lemma
9.34
(ii) we need to consider two cases.
If
A\cap\Pi(x,p_{i})\subset\mathcal{J}
, then this face must be balanced on the scale
N
, since
\Phi_{N}|\mathcal{J}
. Otherwise, we must have
A\cap\Pi(x,p_{i})\subset\mathcal{K}
, and then by the same argument as above, either
\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[x]=\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[x^{\prime}]=p_{k}
or
\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[x]=\mathbb{K}^{N}_{N}[x^{\prime}]=0
. In both cases, the cuboid is balanced, which proves the lemma.
∎

Lemma 9.37
.

Assume (F3) and (
9.85
).
Then

\Phi_{d}|B\ \ \forall d\in\{N_{j},N_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k}^{2}\}.

Proof.

For
d=N_{j}
, this follows from Lemma
9.35
.

Assume, by contradiction, that
\Phi_{N_{k}}|A
. By Lemma
9.8
, we must have
\Phi_{N_{k}}|\mathcal{K}
. As in the proof of Lemma
9.36
, we have
|\mathcal{K}\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})|=p_{j}p_{k}
for all
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}
, with each line
\ell_{k}(x)
for
x\in a_{k}*F_{j}
containing an
M
-fiber in the
p_{k}
direction. In particular,

(9.90)

|\mathcal{K}\cap\ell_{k}(a_{k})|=p_{k}\ \ \forall a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}.

On the other hand, let
a^{\prime}_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}
. Then
\Phi_{N_{k}}|\mathcal{K}
implies one of the following:

•

\mathcal{K}
is not fibered on
\Lambda(a^{\prime}_{k},D(N_{k}))
. By Lemma
9.12
,
there exists an
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}
such that
\mathbb{K}^{N_{k}}_{N_{k}/p_{k}}[x]=\phi(p_{k}^{2})
.

•

\mathcal{K}
is
N_{k}
-fibered on
\Lambda(a^{\prime}_{k},D(N_{k}))
. By Lemma
9.11
(i), it can only be fibered in the
p_{k}
direction.

Both of these are clearly incompatible with (
9.90
). This proves that
\Phi_{N_{k}}|B
.

To prove that
\Phi_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}\nmid A
, let
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}
, and consider
M/p_{j}p_{k}
-cuboids with vertices at
a_{k}
and
x\in\ell_{k}(a_{k})
with
(x-a_{k},M)=M/p_{k}^{2}
.
By (
9.80
) and Lemma
9.34
(ii), we have
\mathbb{A}^{M/p_{j}p_{k}}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}[v]=0
for any cuboid vertex
v
other than
a_{k}
and
x
. Varying
x
as above, we see that in order for all such cuboids to be balanced we must have

|A\cap\Pi(a_{k},p_{i}^{2})|=p_{k}\mathbb{A}^{M/p_{j}p_{k}}_{M/p_{j}p_{k}}[a_{k}].

But this is not possible, since the left side is equal to
p_{j}p_{k}
and the right side is divisible by
p_{k}^{2}
.
The same argument, with the cuboids collapsed further to scale
M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}
, proves that

\Phi_{M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k}}\nmid A
.

Finally, we prove that
\Phi_{M_{k}/p_{j}}\nmid A
. Consider any
M_{k}/p_{j}
-cuboid with one vertex at
a_{k}\in\mathcal{K}\setminus\mathcal{J}
. By the same argument as above, we have
\mathbb{A}^{M_{k}/p_{j}}_{M_{k}/p_{j}}[v]=0
for all vertices
v\neq a_{k}
, hence the cuboid cannot be balanced.
∎

Lemma 9.38
.

Assume (F3) and (
9.85
). Then the conditions of Theorem
2.6
are satisfied in the
p_{i}
direction,
after interchanging
A
and
B
.

Proof.

By assumption,
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|B
. We need to verify that
for every
d
such that
p_{i}^{n_{i}}|d|M
and
\Phi_{d}\nmid B
, we have

(9.91)

\Phi_{d/p_{i}^{\alpha}}|A,\ \ \alpha\in\{1,2\}.

By Lemma
9.37
, it remains to check (
9.91
) for
d\in\{M,M_{j},M_{k}\}
.

•

For
d=M
, (
9.91
) follows from Lemma
9.8
since
\mathcal{I}=\emptyset
.

•

For
d=M_{k}
, we have
\Phi_{M_{k}/p_{i}}\Phi_{p_{j}^{2}}|A
by Lemmas
9.33

and
9.36
.

•

For
d=M_{j}
, if
\Phi_{M_{j}}|B
, there is nothing to prove. If on the other hand
\Phi_{M_{j}}|A
, then
Lemma
9.33
and Lemma
9.36
hold with
j
and
k
interchanged, and so (
9.91
) also holds in this case.

∎

This resolves the case
\Phi_{M_{k}}|A
. The case
\Phi_{M_{j}}|A
is similar, with
j
and
k
interchanged. It remains to prove Proposition
9.4
under the assumption that

(9.92)

\Phi_{M_{\nu}}\nmid A\text{ for }\nu\in\{j,k\}.

Without loss of generality, we may also assume that

(9.93)

p_{k}>p_{j}.

By Corollary
9.13
, this implies that

\Phi_{N_{k}}\Phi_{M_{k}}|B
. It follows that
B
is
\mathcal{T}
-null with respect to the cuboid type
\mathcal{T}=(N_{k},\vec{\delta},1)
, where
\vec{\delta}=(1,1,0)
. Since cuboids of this type are 2-dimensional, it follows by Lemma
4.7
that for every
b\in B
at least one of the following holds:

(9.94)

\mathbb{B}_{M}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{k}}[y]=1\text{ for every }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y,M)=M/p_{j},

(9.95)

\mathbb{B}_{M}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{k}}[y]=1\text{ for every }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y,M)=M/p_{i}.

In particular, this implies that

(9.96)

\{D(M)|m|M\}\cap{\mathrm{Div}}(B)\neq\emptyset.

Lemma 9.39
.

Assume (F3), (
9.92
), and (
9.93
).
Then
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
and
\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
.

Proof.

We start with the second part. Assume for contradiction that

\Phi_{N_{j}}|A
. By Lemma
9.12
applied to
p_{j}
, (
9.96
), and Lemma
9.11
(i),
the set
\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
must be
N_{j}
-fibered in the
p_{j}
direction. Let
a_{j}\in\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
.
We now consider two cases.

•

Suppose that
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
.
By Lemma
9.34
(ii), we have
A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{i})\subset\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
and
|A\cap\Pi(a_{j},p_{i})|=p_{j}p_{k}
.
But then the fibering of
\mathcal{J}\setminus\mathcal{K}
implies that
p_{j}p_{k}
is divisible by
p_{j}^{2}
, a contradiction.

•

Assume now that
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|A
. Let
A^{\prime}
be a translate of
A
such that
a_{j}\in A^{\prime}_{p_{i}}
. By the cyclotomic divisibility assumption, we have
|A^{\prime}_{p_{i}}|=p_{j}p_{k}
. On the other hand, by the fibering properties of
A
,

p_{j}p_{k}=|A^{\prime}_{p_{i}}|=c_{j}p_{j}^{2}+c_{k}p_{k},\ \ c_{j}>0.

Thus
c_{j}=p_{k}c^{\prime}_{j}
and
p_{j}=c^{\prime}_{j}p_{j}^{2}+c_{k}
with
c^{\prime}_{j}>0
, a contradiction.

Therefore
\Phi_{N_{j}}\nmid A
. By (
9.92
), we have
\Phi_{N_{j}}\Phi_{M_{j}}|B
. Applying the same argument as in (
9.94
), (
9.95
) to
p_{j}
instead of
p_{k}
, we get that every
b\in B
must satisfy at least one of

\mathbb{B}_{M}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}}[y]=1\text{ for every }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y,M)=M/p_{k},

(9.97)

\mathbb{B}_{M}[y]+\mathbb{B}_{M/p_{j}}[y]=1\text{ for every }y\in\mathbb{Z}_{M}\text{ with }(b-y,M)=M/p_{i}.

But since
p_{k}>p_{j}
, if the former holds for some
b\in B
, we must have
M/p_{k}\in{\mathrm{Div}}(B)
, which is not allowed. Thus (
9.97
) holds for all
b\in B
. Hence the assumptions of Lemma
4.2
hold for
B
, with
m=M/p_{i}p_{j}
and
s=p_{i}^{2}
. It follows that
\Phi_{p_{i}^{2}}|B
, and therefore
\Phi_{p_{i}}|A
.
∎

Lemma 9.40
.

Assume (F3), (
9.92
), and (
9.93
).
Then the conditions of Theorem
2.6
are satisfied in the
p_{i}
direction,
after interchanging
A
and
B
.

Proof.

We verify the conditions of Theorem
2.6
. By (
9.92
) and Lemma
9.39
, we have

\Phi_{d}|B
for
d\in\{p_{i}^{2},M_{j},M_{k},N_{j}\}
. Next, we claim that

(9.98)

\Phi_{d}|B\hbox{ for }d\in\{M/p_{j}p_{k},M/p_{j}^{2}p_{k},M/p_{j}p_{k}^{2}\}.

Since (
9.97
) holds for all
b\in B
, we may write
B
as

B(X)=B^{i}(X)+Q(X)(X^{M/p_{j}}-1)

for some polynomial
Q(X)
, where
B^{i}
is
M
-fibered in the
p_{i}
direction. By (
9.2
), we have
\Phi_{d}|B^{i}
for all
p_{i}^{2}|d
. Using also that
\Phi_{d}|(X^{M/p_{j}}-1)
for all
d|M/p_{j}
, we get (
9.98
).

Finally, since
\Phi_{M}|A
, we need to prove that
\Phi_{M/p_{i}^{\alpha}}|A
for
\alpha\in\{1,2\}
. Indeed, since
\mathcal{I}
is empty, this follows from Lemma
9.8
.
∎

This concludes the proof of Proposition
9.4
.

10.
